Statistical Properties of 2D Navier-Stokes Equations with time-periodic forcing and degenerate stochastic forcing
Rongchang Liu, Kening Lu

Abstract
. We consider the incompressible 2D Navier-Stokes equations with periodic boundary conditions driven by a deterministic time periodic forcing and a degenerate stochastic forcing. We show that the system possesses a unique ergodic periodic invariant measure which is exponentially mixing under a Wasserstein metric. We also prove the weak law of large numbers for the continuous time inhomogeneous solution process. In addition, we obtain the weak law of large numbers and central limit theorem by restricting the inhomogeneous solution process to periodic times. The results are independent of the strength of the noise and hold true for any value of viscosity with a lower bound ν 1 \nu_{1} characterized by the Grashof number G 1 G_{1} associated with the deterministic forcing. In the laminar case, there is a larger lower bound ν 2 \nu_{2} of the viscosity characterized by the Grashof number G 2 G_{2} associated with both the deterministic and random forcing. We prove that in this laminar case, the system has trivial dynamics for any viscosity larger than ν 2 \nu_{2} by demonstrating the existence of a unique globally exponentially stable random periodic solution that supports the unique periodic invariant measure.



2000 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary:60H15, 37A25, 60F05, 76D05

1.
Introduction

We study the statistic properties of the incompressible 2D Navier-Stokes equations on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}=[-\pi,\pi]^{2}
driven by a deterministic periodic forcing and a degenerate stochastic forcing using the vorticity formulation

(1.1)

\displaystyle dw(t,x)+B(\mathcal{K}w,w)(t,x)dt=\nu\mathrm{\mathrm{\Delta}}w(t,x)dt+f(t,x)dt+GdW(t),\quad t>s,\quad w(s)=w_{0},

where
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
w(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}
denotes the value of the scalar vorticity field,
\mathrm{\mathrm{\Delta}}
is the Laplacian operator with periodic boundary conditions,
B(\mathcal{K}w,w)=(\mathcal{K}w)\cdot\nabla w
is the nonlinear term and
\mathcal{K}w
is the velocity that is recovered from
w
by the Biot-Savart integral operator
\mathcal{K}
. The deterministic initial condition
w_{0}
lies in the state space of system (
1.1
), which is chosen as
H:=\left\{w\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\left(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{R}\right):\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}wdx=0\right\}
, where the norm is denoted by
\left\|\cdot\right\|
and the inner product is
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. We also define the interpolation spaces
H_{s}=\left\{w\in H^{s}\left(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{R}\right):\int wdx=0\right\}
and the corresponding norms
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{s}
by
\|w\|_{s}=\left\|\left(-\mathrm{\Delta}\right)^{s/2}w\right\|
.
Here
W(t)
is a standard
d
dimensional two-sided Brownian motion obtained as follows. Let
W^{\pm}(t)
be two independent standard
d
dimensional Brownian motion, then define

\displaystyle W(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr}W^{+}(t)\text{ , }t\geq 0,\\
W^{-}(-t)\text{ , }t<0.\\
\end{array}\right.

The sample space is denoted by
(\mathrm{\mathrm{\Omega}},\mathcal{F},\mathbf{P})
, where
\mathrm{\Omega}=\left\{\omega\in C(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{d}):\omega(0)=0\right\}
endowed with the compact open topology,
\mathcal{F}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra and
\mathbf{P}
is the Wiener measure associated with the Brownian motion
W
. Denote by
\mathcal{F}_{t}
the filtration of
\sigma
-algebras generated by
W(t)
. The coefficient of the noise is a bounded linear operator
G:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow H_{\infty}:=\bigcap_{s>0}H_{s}
, such that
Ge_{i}=g_{i}
, where
\{e_{i}\}
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
g_{i}\in H_{\infty}
for
i=1,2,\cdots,d
. Then the noise can be expressed as
GW(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}g_{i}W_{i}(t)
. Also
for integer
k\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{B}_{k}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|g_{i}\|_{k}^{2}
be the various norms of the energy input from the noise.

We assume that the deterministic force
f(t,x)
is periodic in
t
with period
\mathcal{T}>0
and
f\in C\left(\mathbb{R},H_{1}\right)\cap\mathrm{L}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{2}\left(\mathbb{R},H_{2}\right)
. The regularity condition imposed on
f
is to ensure the spatial regularity of the solution that is needed to show the asymptotic smoothing effect of the dynamics. Under the above conditions, it is well known that there exists a unique solution
w(t,\omega;s,w_{0})
to equation (
1.1
), which generates a Markov transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}:B_{b}(H)\rightarrow B_{b}(H)
defined by

\mathcal{P}_{s,t}\varphi(w_{0}):=\mathbf{E}[\varphi(w(t;s,w_{0}))],\quad\varphi\in B_{b}(H),

where
B_{b}(H)
is the space of bounded Borel measurable functions on
H
with the supremum norm. We denote the transition probabilities as
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}(w,A):=\mathcal{P}_{s,t}\mathbb{I}_{A}(w)
for
A\in\mathfrak{B}
, the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
H
, where
\mathbb{I}_{A}
is the characteristic function of
A
.
By duality, the transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}
acts on the space
\mathcal{P}(H)
of probability measures on
H
by

\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}\mu(A)=\int_{H}\mathcal{P}_{s,t}(w,A)\mu(dw),\text{ for }\mu\in\mathcal{P}(H),A\in\mathfrak{B}.

In this setting, a map
\mu_{\cdot}:t\rightarrow\mu_{t}
from
\mathbb{R}
to the space
\mathcal{P}(H)
satisfying

(1.2)

\displaystyle\int_{H}\mathcal{P}_{s,t}\varphi(w)\mu_{s}(dw)=\int_{H}\varphi(w)\mu_{t}(dw),\quad s\leq t,

for every
\varphi\in B_{b}(H)
is called an
invariant measure
for
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}
, which is also called an evolution system of meaures
[
19
]
or entrance law
[
15
,
16
]
. It is called a
\mathcal{T}
-periodic invariant measure
if it is invariant and
\mu_{t+\mathcal{T}}=\mu_{t}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
\mu_{t}
is
ergodic
if it is a disintegration of an ergodic invariant measure of the corresponding homogenized Markov semigroup. As it was shown in
[
19
]
, the unique ergodicity of system (
1.1
) is characterized by the uniqueness of the continuous
\mathcal{T}
-periodic invariant measure
\mu_{t}
for
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}
, where the continuity of
\mu_{t}
is defined by endowing
\mathcal{P}(H)
with the topology of weak convergence.

In this paper, we show the unique ergodicity and mixing properties of the periodic invariant measure for system (
1.1
). We also prove the weak law of large numbers for the time inhomogeneous solution process of system (
1.1
) and show the weak law of large numbers and central limit theorem for the restriction of the time inhomogeneous solution process to periodic times. To state our main results, as in
[
4
,
36
]
, we define recursively the following sets
\{A_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
formed by the symmetrized nonlinear term
\widetilde{B}(u,w)=-B(\mathcal{K}u,w)-B(\mathcal{K}w,u)
. Set
A_{1}=\{g_{l}:1\leq l\leq d\}
,
A_{k+1}=A_{k}\cup\{\widetilde{B}(h,g_{l}):h\in A_{k},g_{l}\in A_{1}\}
, and
A_{\infty}=\overline{\mathrm{span}(\cup_{k\geq 1}A_{k})}
. These sets reflect how noise propagates to the state space through the nonlinear term, which plays an important role when proving the asymptotic regularizing effect of the transition operator. For
\eta>0
small, recall the metric
\rho
on
H
weighted by the Lyapunov function
e^{\eta\|w\|^{2}}
as in
[
35
]
,

(1.3)

\displaystyle\rho(w_{1},w_{2})=\inf_{\gamma}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\eta\|\gamma(t)\|^{2}}\|\dot{\gamma}(t)\|dt,\quad\forall w_{1},w_{2}\in H,

where the infimum is taken over all differentiable path
\gamma
connecting
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
.

This metric naturally induces a Wasserstein metric on
\mathcal{P}(H)
by

\rho(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{C}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\int_{H\times H}\rho(u,v)\mu(dudv),

where
\mathcal{C}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
is the set of couplings of
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(H)
. The subset

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{1}(H):=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(H):\rho(\mu,\delta_{0})<\infty\}

is complete under the metric
\rho

[
8
,
55
]
, where
\delta_{0}
is the Dirac measure at
0
. Denote by
C(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{P}_{1}(H))
the set of all continuous maps from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)
.
Let
c_{0}
be the constant from the Ladyzhenskaya’s inequality

||w||_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{2}\leq c_{0}\|w\|_{1}\|w\|.

Let
\displaystyle\|f\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\|f(t)\|
, and define the Grashof number
G_{1}=\|f\|_{\infty}/\nu^{2}
associated with the deterministic equation corresponding to system (
1.1
). Also let
G_{2}=\sqrt{G_{1}^{2}+\mathcal{B}_{0}/\nu^{3}}
be the Grashof number for the whole system (
1.1
). These two different numbers characterize different scales of the viscosity for which system (
1.1
) has different dynamics.

Our main results are summarized as the following:

Main Theorem
.

(1)

Assume
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
.

(i)

If
A_{\infty}=H
, then the system (
1.1
) has a unique
\mathcal{T}
-periodic invariant measure
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\in C(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{P}_{1}(H))
, which is exponentially mixing in the sense that, under the Wasserstein metric
\rho
, any trajectory of
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}
acting on
\mathcal{P}(H)
is exponentially attracted by the periodic path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
.

(ii)

There are two cases in which
A_{\infty}=H
can fail as in
[
34
]
. In each case there is a unique exponentially mixing
\mathcal{T}
-periodic invariant measure supported on
A_{\infty}
when
A_{\infty}
is an invariant subspace of the system (
1.1
).

(iii)

In the above case (i) and (ii), the weak law of large numbers for the continuous time inhomogeneous solution process holds. Also the weak law of large numbers and central limit theorem hold when restricting the inhomogeneous solution process to periodic times.

(2)

If
G_{2}<1/c_{0}
, then the unique
\mathcal{T}
-periodic invariant measure
\mu_{t}
is supported on a unique random periodic solution of equation (
1.1
), that exponentially attracts any bounded set in
H
. In words, the dynamics is trivial in this case. Here the random periodic solution is periodic in a path wise manner defined by the stochastic flow (or random dynamical system) generated by the solution map of system (
1.1
), see
[
29
]
.

A sufficient condition on the driven noise that guarantees
A_{\infty}=H
was characterized by Hairer and Mattingly in
[
34
]
. In particular, the noise does not need to act on all determining modes and can be extremely degenerate, for example it is allowed to excite only in four directions. We stress here that part
(1)
of the Main Theorem does not depend on the strength of the noise and holds true for any viscosity satisfying
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
, which is purely deterministic.

In the case when
f=0
, the unique ergodicity was proved in
[
34
]
, and the weak law of large numbers and the central limit theorem for continuous time homogeneous solution process was shown in
[
44
]
. When
f
is nonzero and independent of time, the exponentially mixing property was shown in
[
35
]
under the range condition
f\in\mathrm{range}(G)
. This range condition allows one to apply the Girsanov’s fromula to
reduce the problem of the weak irreducibility for nonzero
f\in\mathrm{range}(G)
to the case
f=0
that has been proved in
[
34
]
. In the absence of the range condition, we believe that the condition on the Grashof number is necessary to obtain our results in part
(1)
. Since even in the case that
f
is independent of time, when the Grashof number
G_{1}\geq 1/c_{0}
is large, the dynamics on the attractor of the deterministic problem could become complicated
[
10
,
11
,
12
]
, hence if there is no range condition, the noise is absent in certain directions on which the force
f
acts, so that the effects of the force
f
may not be compensated by the noise instantly and system (
1.1
) might fail to be uniquely ergodic.

Comparing the two parts of the Main Theorem, we see that the dynamics under the condition
G_{1}<1/c_{0}\leq G_{2}
is uniquely ergodic and mixing but the structure of the attractor remains unknown. When
f\equiv 0
, this condition becomes
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
for some
\nu_{0}
determined by
c_{0}
and
\mathcal{B}_{0}
as above. And the comparison becomes that of the results from
[
34
,
35
]
and
[
46
]
, where the dynamics is uniquely ergodic and mixing for
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
but the structure of the attractor is expected to be nontrivial for sufficiently small value of viscosity
[
28
,
34
]
. It is worthwhile to mention that in the theory of dynamical systems, there are indeed examples of uniquely ergodic system such as horocycle flow that have chaotic behavior. Therefore the unique ergodicity does not necessarily imply that the system has a trivial attractor and the dynamics of system (
1.1
) are expected to be complicated if
G_{1}<1/c_{0}\leq G_{2}
.

In the case when the deterministic force is independent of
t
, i.e.,
f(t,x)\equiv f(x)
, the solution of equation (
1.1
) forms a time homogeneous Markov process, which generates a Markov semigroup
(\mathcal{P}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
.
In this context, the statistical properties of the stochastic Navier-Stokes system have been extensively studied over the decades, see for example
[
1
,
2
,
22
,
23
,
25
,
26
,
33
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
48
,
49
,
51
,
54
]
and references therein. However, the results in the cited papers require the random forcing to act on all determining modes. In particular, the dimension of the random forcing goes to infinity as viscosity approaches to zero. A major breakthrough was made by Hairer and Mattingly in their seminal work
[
34
]
, where they introduced the asymptotic strong Feller property to show unique ergodicity of the Navier-Stokes equation driven by an extremely degenerate noise. To obtain this asymptotic smoothing property, they developed a theory of infinite dimensional Malliavin calculus and established an infinite dimensional Hörmander type theorem. This asymptotic smoothing effect also allowed them to develop an infinite dimensional Harris-like theorem in
[
35
]
, to prove the exponential convergence to the unique invariant measure under the Wasserstein metric
\rho
. The result in
[
35
]
in turn led to a proof of the weak law of large numbers and the central limit theorem in
[
44
]
for non-stationary Markov processes. It is notable that the statistical results in
[
34
,
35
,
44
]
are independent of the strength of the noise and the viscosity but require a range condition
f\in\mathrm{range}(G)
when the time independent force
f(x)
is nonzero. Our Main Theorem also implies the same statistical results for equation (
1.1
) with a time independent deterministic forcing
f(x)
without assuming the range condition, but requiring the condition
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
on the Grashof number.

A natural question is then whether the similar results hold for equation (
1.1
) with a nonzero deterministic force
f(t,x)
that depends on
t
.
The setting in this case has a different flavor since the solution is a time inhomogeneous Markov process, which generates a Markov transition operator
(\mathcal{P}_{s,t})_{s\leq t}
instead of a semigroup. And the concept of an invariant measure is replaced by a family of measures defined by equation (
1.2
). The first result in this direction was obtained by Da Prato and Debussche in
[
19
]
. By generalizing the theorem of Doob and Khasminskii and applying a coupling argument, they proved the unique ergodicity and exponentially mixing for system (
1.1
), provided that the driven noise is nondegenerate. They also generalized the concept of asymptotic strong Feller property in
[
34
]
to the time inhomogeneous setting. But the irreducibility condition proposed there is not easy to verify so that the unique ergodicity under extremely degenerate noise remains unproved. To the best of our knowledge, there is no results analogue to those in
[
34
,
35
,
44
]
for system (
1.1
) with an extremely degenerate noise. The present work is an attempt to give such results in the time inhomogeneous setting for equation (
1.1
).

To obtain our main results, we take an approach that is different from
[
19
]
. Our approach is mainly inspired by the Harris-like theorem for infinite dimensional systems developed in
[
35
]
, the theory of non-autonomous dynamical systems
[
7
,
13
,
14
]
especially on the structure of attractors for evolution equations, and the limit theorems for additive functionals of non-stationary homogeneous Markov processes
[
44
]
.

To be specific, we first show that under the Wasserstein metric
\rho
, the transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}
has a strong contraction property uniform in the initial time
s
when acting on
\mathcal{P}(H)
. Then, we characterize
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}
as a cocycle over the hull
H(f)
of the periodic force
f(t,x)
equipped with the Bebutov shift flow. Due to the Lyapunov structure, we only know that the cocycle is continuous in
h\in H(f)
when acting on those probability measures under which the Lyapunov function is integrable. Therefore we choose to work with the closed subsets
\mathcal{P}_{R}
of
\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)
consisting of those measures for which the integral of the Lyapunov function is uniformly bounded by
R>0
. Then by a pull back procedure we associate the cocycle with a semigroup
S^{t}
acting on
C(H(f),\mathcal{P}_{R})
, the space of continuous mappings from
H(f)
to
\mathcal{P}_{R}
, whose fixed point gives the desired
\mathcal{T}
-periodic invariant measure. However, for fixed
R>0
, the semigroup
S^{t}
is not necessarily contracting on
C(H(f),\mathcal{P}_{R})
for all
t>0
. As
t
becomes smaller, we need larger
R
to ensure that
S^{t}
is contracting on
C(H(f),\mathcal{P}_{R})
. Hence we choose to apply the fixed point theorem on a family of nested closed subsets instead of the whole space
C(H(f),\mathcal{P}_{1}(H))
, and show the existence of a common periodic invariant measure in all of the nested closed subsets. The uniqueness and exponentially mixing then follows simultaneously from the contraction of
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}
on
\mathcal{P}(H)
and the invariance of the periodic invariant measure.

The limit theorems are proved after one shows the strong contraction of
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}
. Here we first prove a weak law of large numbers for the continuous time inhomogeneous solution process with arbitrary initial condition. However it is unclear whether a central limit theorem holds or not for the continuous time inhomogeneous solution process. Fortunately the idea of the Poincare section behind the periodic structure enables us to show the weak law of large numbers and the central limit theorem by considering the restriction of the solution process to periodic times. In particular, the proof for the central limit theorem follows from a modified martingale approximation approach from
[
44
]
, where the idea dates back to the early works of Gordin
[
31
]
, Kipnis and Varadhan
[
38
]
.

The proof for the contraction of
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}
on
\mathcal{P}(H)
involves several uniform in initial time estimates that reflect the contraction of the dynamics at different scales. Among those estimates, one is on the weak irreducibility that was introduced in
[
35
]
, and here we adapt it to our time inhomogeneous and periodic setting.
The weak irreducibility is a property about the existence of an accessible point
v
in the state space such that for any neighborhood of
v
, there is a positive probability for the solutions starting from different initial points to enter into that neighborhood at some large time. For system (
1.1
), when
f(t,x)=0
, the accessible point is the global stable equilibrium point of the corresponding deterministic system
[
34
]
. If the deterministic force
f(t,x)\equiv f(x)
is nonzero and time independent, then one can reduce the problem to the case when
f(x)=0
as long as
f(x)
satisfies the range condition
[
35
]
. When the nonzero deterministic forcing
f(t,x)
depends on
t
, the range condition is indeed much more restrictive, hence we do not require the range condition on the periodic force
f(t,x)
, but the trade off is the constraint
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
on the Grashof number. Under this constraint, there is a unique global stable periodic solution
[
14
]
of the deterministic equation corresponding to (
1.1
). This allows us to show the weak irreducibility for the time inhomogeneous system (
1.1
) since the stable periodic solution is accessible in this case.
As a byproduct, we show that this type of irreducibility condition, together with the asymptotic strong Feller property proposed in
[
19
]
that is verified in our work, gives an applicable Doob and Khasminskii type argument that shows system (
1.1
) can only have at most one periodic invariant measure.
Another two estimates are the Lyapunov structure that is used to compensate the non-uniformity of the dynamics of equation (
1.1
) and the gradient inequality that reveals the asymptotic regularizing effect of the transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}
.
Since the estimates about the solution of equation (
1.1
) are uniform in the initial time, hence by carrying over the proof from
[
35
]
and
[
36
]
, we obtain uniform in the initial time estimates for the Lyapunov structure and gradients inequality.

In applications, it is more convenient to represent the mixing result by the action of transition operator on observables. In the time homogeneous case as in
[
35
]
, the exponentially mixing that involves observables is given by proving the quasi-equivalence of a particular norm
\|\cdot\|_{\eta}
on the observables with an appropriate norm
\|\cdot\|_{\rho,\mu}
on the space of Lipschitz functions on
H
under the metric
\rho
, where the later norm depends on the invariant measure
\mu
. While in the present work, the periodic invariant measure
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
depends on time. Hence we choose a family of norms
\{\|\cdot\|_{\rho,\mu_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{R}}
on the space of Lipschitz functions that depends on
\rho
and the values of the periodic invariant measure at initial times. The mixing involving observables in our time inhomogeneous case is then obtained by showing the quasi-equivalence of
\|\cdot\|_{\eta}
with
\|\cdot\|_{\rho,\mu_{s}}
under the transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Here the dependence on initial time of the norm can be regarded as a property adapted to the time inhomogeneity, to yield a uniform contraction under the transition operator.

The proof for the second part of our Main Theorem is based on the approach from
[
46
]
of constructing the unique attracting stationary solution, where the author used the contraction property of the dynamics on the whole state space when the viscosity is large. We will use the contraction property as well, which is the case if
G_{2}<1/c_{0}
. The main difference is that we need to incorporate the periodic structure here to construct the unique random periodic solution. Note when
f(t,x)=0
, the second part of our theorem becomes the result proved by Mattingly in
[
46
]
.

2.
Preliminaries and main results

Under the conditions given in the introduction, the existence and uniqueness of the solution to equation (
1.1
) is well known, see for example
[
4
]
and references therein. To be specific, for any initial time
s\in\mathbb{R},T>s
and any
w_{0}\in H
, equation (
1.1
) has a unique strong solution
w(t,\omega;s,w_{0})
, adapted to the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, which generates a stochastic flow
\mathrm{\Phi}(t,\omega;s,\cdot):H\rightarrow H
such that
\mathrm{\Phi}(t,\omega;s,w_{0})=w(t,\omega;s,w_{0})
for
s\leq t,w_{0}\in H
and

w\in C\left([s,T];H\right)\cap C\left((s,T];H_{3}\right),\,\,\mathbf{P}\text{-}\mathrm{a.s}..

Here by a stochastic flow
\mathrm{\Phi}(t,\omega;s,w_{0})
, we mean that it is a modification of the solution of equation (
1.1
) satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

It is adapted to
\mathcal{F}_{t}
and for almost all
\omega
,
\mathrm{\Phi}(t,\omega;s,w_{0})
is continuous in
(t,s,w_{0})
and
\mathrm{\Phi}(s,\omega;s,w_{0})=w_{0}.

(2)

For almost all
\omega
,

\mathrm{\Phi}(t+\tau,\omega;s,w_{0})=\mathrm{\Phi}(t+\tau,\omega;t,\mathrm{\Phi}(t,\omega;s,w_{0})),

for
s\leq t
,
\tau>0
and
w_{0}\in H
.

Throughout the work, we will also use
w_{s,t}(\omega,w_{0})
or
\Phi_{s,t}(\omega,w_{0})
to represent the solution
w(t,\omega;s,w_{0})
.

A random periodic solution of period
\mathcal{T}
for system (
1.1
) is a stochastic process
w^{*}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathrm{\Omega}\rightarrow H
that is progressively measurable and satisfies

(2.1)

\displaystyle w^{*}(t+\mathcal{T},\omega)=w^{*}(t,\theta_{\mathcal{T}}\omega)\text{ and }\mathrm{\Phi}(\tau,\omega;t,w^{*}(t,\omega))=w^{*}(\tau,\omega),

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\tau\geq t
and almost every
\omega
. Here
\theta:\mathbb{R}\times\mathrm{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathrm{\Omega}
given by

\theta_{t}\omega(\cdot):=\omega(t+\cdot)-\omega(t)

is the Wiener shift.

2.1.
Periodic invariant measures

By the well-posedness of equation (
1.1
), one can define the associated Feller transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}\left(s\leq t\right)
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{s,t}\varphi(w_{0})=\mathbf{E}[\varphi(w(t;s,w_{0}))],\quad\varphi\in B_{b}(H).

It was shown in
[
19
]
that this transition operator is a
\mathcal{T}
-periodic evolution operator, i.e.,
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}=\mathcal{P}_{s,r}\mathcal{P}_{r,t}
and
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}=\mathcal{P}_{s+\mathcal{T},t+\mathcal{T}}
for all
s\leq r\leq t
. Following
[
17
,
19
]
, it is natural to consider the invariant measure for
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}
as a map
\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto\mu_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(H)
, such that for every
\varphi\in B_{b}(H)
,

\displaystyle\int_{H}\mathcal{P}_{s,t}\varphi(w)\mu_{s}(dw)=\int_{H}\varphi(w)\mu_{t}(dw),\quad s\leq t,

or equivalently

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}\mu_{s}=\mu_{t},\quad s\leq t.

Given
\mathcal{T}>0
, it is called
\mathcal{T}
-periodic if

\mu_{t+\mathcal{T}}=\mu_{t},\quad t\in\mathbb{R}.

2.2.
Main results

In this subsection, we formulate the main results of the present paper in details. Recall that
\mathcal{P}(H)
is the space of probability measures on
H
. For
\eta>0,0<r\leq 1
, define the metric
\rho_{r}
on
H
weighted by the Lyapunov function
V(w)=e^{\eta\|w\|^{2}}
as in
[
35
]
,

(2.2)

\displaystyle\rho_{r}(w_{1},w_{2})=\inf_{\gamma}\int_{0}^{1}V^{r}(\gamma(t))\|\dot{\gamma}(t)\|dt,\quad\forall w_{1},w_{2}\in H,

where the infimum is taken over all differentiable path
\gamma
in
H
connecting
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
. The family of metrics
\rho_{r}
will be used in Section
3
. When
r=1
we have
\rho_{r}=\rho
as given in (
1.3
). Denote by
\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(H)
the space of Lipschitz functions on
H
endowed with the metric
\rho
and by
\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(\phi)
the Lipschitz constant for
\phi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(H)
. The metric naturally induces a Wasserstein metric on
\mathcal{P}(H)
by

\rho(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{C}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\int_{H\times H}\rho(u,v)\mu(dudv),

where
\mathcal{C}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
is the set of couplings of
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(H)
.
The reason for working with the Wasserstein metric is that the transition probabilities in infinite dimensional systems are likely to be mutually singular, especially when the strong Feller property does not hold
[
35
]
. Hence the convergence to the invariant measure often fails under the total variation metric and one would like to replace it by a weaker Wasserstein metric.
For
\eta>0
, let
C_{\eta}^{1}:=\{\phi\in C^{1}(H):\|\phi\|_{\eta}<\infty\}
, where

(2.3)

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{\eta}:=\sup_{w\in H}e^{-\eta\|w\|^{2}}\big(|\phi(w)|+\|\nabla\phi(w)\|\big).

Recall from the introduction that the set
A_{\infty}
is defined by setting
A_{1}=\{g_{l}:1\leq l\leq d\}
,
A_{k+1}=A_{k}\cup\{\widetilde{B}(h,g_{l}):h\in A_{k},g_{l}\in A_{1}\}
, and
A_{\infty}=\overline{\mathrm{span}(\cup_{k\geq 1}A_{k})}
, where
\widetilde{B}(u,w)=-B(\mathcal{K}u,w)-B(\mathcal{K}w,u)
is the the symmetrized nonlinear term.

The following Theorem
2.1
-
2.3
contain the results for the case when
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
and
A_{\infty}=H
, where system (
1.1
) is uniquely ergodic and mixing on the whole space. The case when
A_{\infty}=H
fails is formulated in Theorem
2.6
, where the periodic invariant measure is unique and mixing when restricting system (
1.1
) on the proper subspace
A_{\infty}
. The laminar case
G_{2}<1/c_{0}
where system (
1.1
) has trivial dynamics is given in Theorem
2.4
. Note that the metric
\rho
weighted by the Lyapunov function
V(w)=e^{\eta\|w\|^{2}}
depends on the parameter
\eta>0
.

The first result is the following unique ergodicity and exponentially mixing of the periodic invariant measure under the Wasserstein metric.

Theorem 2.1
.

(Ergodicity and mixing)
Assume
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
,
A_{\infty}=H
. Then there is a constant
\eta_{0}>0
, such that for every
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, there exist constants
C,\gamma>0
independent of initial time
s
, and a unique
\mathcal{T}
-periodic path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\in C(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{P}_{1}(H))
such that
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}\mu_{s}=\mu_{t}
and

(2.4)

\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}^{*}\mu,\mu_{s+t})\leq Ce^{-\gamma t}\rho(\mu,\mu_{s}),\quad\forall s\in\mathbb{R},t\geq 0,\mu\in\mathcal{P}(H).

In an equivalent form that involves the transition operator acting on observables, we have for every
\phi\in C_{\eta}^{1}
,

(2.5)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi-\int_{H}\phi(w)\mu_{s+t}(dw)\|_{\eta}\leq Ce^{-\gamma t}\|\phi\|_{\eta}.

Theorem
2.1
will be proved in Section
3
, by combining a fixed point argument with the following contraction property proved in the same section.

Theorem 2.2
.

(
Contraction on
\mathcal{P}(H)
)
Assume
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
,
A_{\infty}=H
. Then there exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that for
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, there are positive constants
C
and
\gamma
such that

(2.6)

\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}^{*}\mu_{1},\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}^{*}\mu_{2})\leq Ce^{-\gamma t}\rho(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}),

for every
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
t\geq 0
, and any
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(H)
.

The second result is the weak law of large numbers (WLLN) and the central limit theorem (CLT) for the solution process. The proof will be given in Section
4
. Note that the restriction of the solution process starting at
(s,w_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times H
to periodic times
\{s+n\mathcal{T}\}_{n\geq 0}
forms a time homogeneous Markov chain
\{w_{s,s+n\mathcal{T}}(w_{0})\}_{n\geq 0}
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Assume
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
,
A_{\infty}=H
. Then we have the following:

1. (WLLN for the time inhomogeneous solution process) For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
w_{0}\in H
,

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\psi(w_{s,s+t}(w_{0}))dt=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}}\int_{H}\psi(w)\mu_{s}(dw)\lambda(ds)

in probability. Here
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}=\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{T}\mathbb{Z}
, and
\lambda
is the normalized Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}
.

2. (WLLN for the Markov chain) For any
s\in\mathbb{R},w_{0}\in H
,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\psi\left(w_{s,s+k\mathcal{T}}(w_{0})\right)=\int_{H}\psi(w)\mu_{s}(dw)

in probability.

3. (CLT for the Markov chain) Let
\widetilde{\psi}_{s}(w):=\psi(w)-\int_{H}\psi(w)\mu_{s}(dw)
. Then for any
s\in\mathbb{R},w_{0}\in H
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\widetilde{\psi}_{s}\left(w_{s,s+k\mathcal{T}}(w_{0})\right)<\xi\right)=\Phi_{\sigma}(\xi),\quad\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R},

where
\Phi_{\sigma}(\cdot)
is the distribution function of a normal random variable with variance
\sigma^{2}
, and

\sigma^{2}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\widetilde{\psi}_{s}\left(w_{s,s+k\mathcal{T}}(w_{0})\right)\right]^{2},

where
\sigma:=\sigma(s)
is
\mathcal{T}
-periodic in
s
.

The third result shows that if the viscosity is larger than that in the previous two results, then the dynamics of the system (
1.1
) is actually trivial. The following theorem will be proved in Section
5
.

Theorem 2.4
.

If
G_{2}<1/c_{0}
, then there exists a random
\mathcal{T}
-periodic solution
w^{*}(t,x)
of equation (
1.1
) in the sense of the definition given by equation (
2.1
). Moreover,
w^{*}(t,x)
exponentially attracts all other solutions both in forward and pullback times. The law of
w^{*}(t,x)
gives the unique
\mathcal{T}
-periodic invariant measure.

We make remarks on the above results.

First of all, the driven noise here can be extremely degenerate as in
[
34
]
, where Hairer and Mattingly give an algebraic characterization of the condition
A_{\infty}=H
. To state this characterization we recall the following real Fourier basis of
H
consisting of eigenvectors of the Laplacian as in
[
34
,
50
]
. Set
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}=\left\{\left(k_{1},k_{2}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\,\middle|\,k_{2}>0\right\}\cup\left\{\left(k_{1},0\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\,\middle|\,k_{1}>0\right\}
,
\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{2}=-\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{0}^{2}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}\cup\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{2}
. And define for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{0}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sin(k\cdot x)&\text{ if }k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2},\\
\cos(k\cdot x)&\text{ if }k\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{2}.\end{array}\right.

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{0}:=\{k_{l}:1\leq l\leq d_{0}\}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{0}^{2}
, where
d_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
. The following theorem was proved in
[
34
]
:

Theorem 2.5
.

[
34
]

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
satisfy the following conditions:

A1. There exist at least two elements in
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
with different Euclidean norms.

A2. Integer linear combinations of elements of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
generate
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

If
\{\gamma_{k}:k\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}\}\subset\mathrm{span}\{g_{i}:1\leq i\leq d\}
, then
A_{\infty}=H
.

In particular, the random forcing is allowed to excite only on four directions. For example, one can take
\mathcal{Z}_{0}=\{(1,0),(-1,0),(1,1),(-1,-1)\}
.

The case when
A_{\infty}\neq H
is best characterized by
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
as in
[
34
]
. To do so, we set
Q_{1}=\{\gamma_{l}:l\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}\}
,
Q_{j+1}=Q_{j}\cup\{\widetilde{B}(h,\gamma_{l}):h\in Q_{j},\gamma_{l}\in Q_{1}\}
and
Q_{\infty}=\overline{\mathrm{span}(\cup_{j\geq 1}Q_{j})}
. If the action of the noise is diagonal, then
A_{\infty}
coincides with
Q_{\infty}
. However the noise here does not necessarily act on system (
1.1
) in a diagonal way, therefore we assume
A_{\infty}=Q_{\infty}
in order to characterize
A_{\infty}
by
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and ensure the invariance of
A_{\infty}
. Then there are two cases in which
A_{\infty}=H
can fail
[
34
]
:

(1)

Case 1
: The elements of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
are all collinear or of the same Euclidean length. Then
A_{\infty}
is the finite-dimensional space spanned by
\left\{\gamma_{k}\,\middle|\,k\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}\right\}
.

(2)

Case 2
: Let
\mathcal{G}
be the smallest subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
containing
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Then
A_{\infty}
is the space spanned by
\left\{\gamma_{k}\,\middle|\,k\in\mathcal{G}\backslash\{(0,0)\}\right\}.
Let
k_{1},k_{2}
be two generators for
\mathcal{G}
and define
v_{i}=2\pi k_{i}/\left|k_{i}\right|^{2},
then
A_{\infty}
is the space of functions that are periodic with respect to the translations
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.

In this situation, we have the following theorem, which is a counterpart of Theorem 2.3 from
[
34
]
in our time inhomogeneous setting.

Theorem 2.6
.

Assume
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
,
A_{\infty}=Q_{\infty}
and
f(t)\in A_{\infty}
for
t\in[0,\mathcal{T}]
. Then in each of the above two cases,
A_{\infty}
is a closed proper linear subspace of
H
which is invariant under system (
1.1
). When restricting the dynamics of equation (
1.1
) on
A_{\infty}
, there is a unique
T
-periodic invariant measure supported on
A_{\infty}
, for which Theorem
2.1
-
2.4
hold.

In particular, in
Case 1
the dynamics restricted to
A_{\infty}
is that of a periodic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process
[
18
]
.

Remark 2.1
.

The condition
A_{\infty}=Q_{\infty}
enables us to characterize
A_{\infty}
by
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Moreover, by rewriting equation (
1.1
) in Fourier space
[
34
]
, and combining with the assumption
f(t)\in A_{\infty}
for
t\in[0,\mathcal{T}]
, the condition
A_{\infty}=Q_{\infty}
also ensures the invariance of
A_{\infty}
under the dynamics of system (
1.1
). By considering equation (
1.1
) on the invariant subspace
A_{\infty}
, Theorem
2.6
follows from the same arguments for proving corresponding results in the case
A_{\infty}=H
. If
A_{\infty}\neq Q_{\infty}
, then in general we do not know if
A_{\infty}
is invariant and there is no results similar to Theorem
2.6
.

Secondly, once we have established the contraction (
2.2
) of the transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}
, then the existence, uniqueness and mixing of the periodic invariant measure follows simultaneously by a fixed point argument in Theorem
3.2
. In the time inhomogeneous setting, this is different from the approach in
[
19
]
. The authors there showed the existence by first applying the Krylov-Bogoliubov procedure to the associated homogenized Markov semigroup, and then obtained the periodic invariant measure as a continuous version of the disintegration of a invariant measure for the homogenized Markov semigroup. They also generalized the concept of asymptotic strong Feller property to the time inhomogeneous case and established a Doob and Khasminskii type argument to show the uniqueness of periodic invariant measure. Unfortunately the irreducibile type condition proposed there is not easy to verify for system (
1.1
). As a byproduct of the present work, we verified the asymptotic strong Feller property in Corollary
6.17
from subsection
6.3
, and by introducing a type of weak irreducibility in subsection
3.2
, we provide a Doob and Khasminskii type argument to show that system (
1.1
) has at most one periodic invariant measure, see Corollary
3.12
.

Lastly, we prove both WLLN and CLT for the corresponding time homogeneous Markov chain, but only WLLN for the time inhomogeneous solution process. It seems not easy to prove or even formulate a CLT for the continuous time inhomogeneous solution process. We leave this as a future work.

3.
Contraction on
\mathcal{P}(H)
: ergodicity and mixing

In this section, we will prove Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
2.2
. More precisely, we first show Theorem
2.1
in subsection
3.1
by assuming the contraction result of Theorem
2.2
. The proof is accomplished by viewing the transition operator as a non-autonomous dynamical system on
\mathcal{P}(H)
and employing a fixed point argument. This gives the unique ergodicity and mixing of the Navier-Stokes system (
1.1
).

The proof for the contraction (
2.2
) in Theorem
2.2
will be given in subsection
3.3
, after we establish the necessary estimates in our time inhomogeneous setting. In particular, we will prove the weak irreducibility in
3.2
.

3.1.
Ergodicity and mixing by contraction

In this subsection, we will prove Theorem
2.1
by assuming the contraction (
2.2
) of Theorem
2.2
. In the following Theorem
3.2
, we show the existence of a unique
\mathcal{T}
-periodic invariant measure that has the mixing property (
2.4
). The mixing (
2.5
) in terms of the action on observables is given in Theorem
3.4
below.

In the time homogeneous case
[
35
]
, if the Markov semigroup is a contraction on
\mathcal{P}(H)
, the existence of a unique invariant measure follows directly by applying the fixed point theorem on the complete subset
\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)
defined as (
1.4
). However, this is not an obvious task when considering the time inhomogeneous Markov transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}
. Inspired by the work in
[
7
]
and
[
13
]
on non-autonomous dynamical systems, we consider the transition operator as a cocycle over the hull
H(f)
of the periodic force
f
equipped with the Bebutov shift flow. Then we associate the cocycle with a semigroup acting on a family of nested closed subsets of
C\left(H(f),\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)\right)
, whose fixed point is the periodic invariant measure for
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}
. As we mentioned in the introduction, due to the Lyapunov structure, the contraction of the semigroup is not uniform on the whole complete space
C\left(H(f),\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)\right)
. Hence we choose to apply the fixed point theorem on a family of nested closed subsets instead of the whole space
C(H(f),\mathcal{P}_{1}(H))
to adapte to this non-uniformity.
In what follows we will elaborate the ideas and give the proof for Theorem
3.2
.

We first state a result which shows that the system (
1.1
) has a uniform in the initial time Lyapunov structure , which was first introduced in
[
35
]
in a time homogeneous setting. Since our estimates about the solution of equation (
1.1
) is uniform in the initial time, the proof of the Lyapunov structure in
[
35
]
carries over to the time inhomogeneous setting here. For the sake of completeness, we give the proof in Appendix
6.2
.

Proposition 3.1

(The Lyapunov Structure)
.

Let
V(w)=\exp(\eta\|w\|)
. Then there exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, we have the following

1. There are
\kappa>1
and
C>0
such that
\|w\|V(w)\leq CV^{\kappa}(w)
for all
w\in H
.

2. There is a constant
C>0
such that for any fixed
0<r_{0}<1

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}V^{r}(\Phi_{s,s+t}(w))(1+\|\nabla\Phi_{s,s+t}(w))\xi\|)\leq CV^{r\alpha(t)}(w)

for every
w,\xi\in H
with
\|\xi\|=1
,
r\in[r_{0},2\kappa]
and every
t\in[0,\mathcal{T}],s\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\alpha(t)=e^{-\frac{\nu}{4}t}.

3. There is a constant
C
(possibly larger) such that

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}V^{r}(\Phi_{s,s+t}(w))\leq CV^{r\alpha(t)}(w),

for every
t\geq 0
and
r\in[r_{0},2\kappa]
. Here
\alpha(t)=\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{k}\alpha(\beta)
is an extension to
\mathbb{R}
of the function
\alpha(t)
in (
3.1
), where
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\beta\in[0,\mathcal{T})
are unique numbers such that
t=k\mathcal{T}+\beta
.

We view the stochastic flow generated by equation (
1.1
) as a family of systems over the hull
H(f):=\mathrm{cl}\{f(s+\cdot)|s\in\mathbb{R}\}
of the time dependent force
f
, where the closure is taking with respect to the uniform convergence topology on
C_{b}(\mathbb{R},H)
, the space of bounded continuous maps. For each fixed
u\in H(f)
, there is a stochastic flow
\Phi_{s,t,u}(w_{0})
obtained by solving the equation

\displaystyle dw(t,x)+B(\mathcal{K}w,w)(t,x)dt=\nu\mathrm{\mathrm{\Delta}}w(t,x)dt+u(t,x)dt+GdW(t),\quad t>s,\quad w(s)=w_{0},

which in turn induces a transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,t,u}\phi(w_{0}):=\mathbf{E}[\phi(\Phi_{s,t,u}(w_{0}))]
indexed by
u
. Here we consider the case when
f
is
\mathcal{T}
-periodic, so
H(f)
can be identified with the circle
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}=\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{T}\mathbb{Z}=[0,\mathcal{T})
of length
\mathcal{T}
, where each
y\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}
is identified with
f(y+\cdot)
. We denote by
\sigma
the rotation over the circle
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}
induced from the Bebutov shift on the hull
H(f)
. It is defined by
\sigma(t)y=t+y\bmod\mathcal{T}
for
y\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that for
y=0
, the transition operator is
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}
corresponding to
f
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Assume
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
,
A_{\infty}=H
. Then there is a constant
\eta_{0}>0
, such that for every
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, there is a unique
\mathcal{T}
-periodic path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\in C(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{P}_{1}(H))
that is invariant, i.e.,
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}\mu_{s}=\mu_{t}
and there exist constant
C,\gamma>0
such that

(3.3)

\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}\nu,\mu_{t})\leq Ce^{-\gamma(t-s)}\rho(\nu,\mu_{s}),

for any
s\leq t
and
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(H)
. Also
\int_{H}\exp\left(2\kappa\eta\|w\|^{2}\right)\mu_{s}(dw)\leq C
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\kappa\geq 1
is a constant.

Proof.

We first show that the transition operator satisfies the following translation identity, which is a conclusion by the uniqueness of solution:

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\tau+h,t+h,y}=\mathcal{P}_{\tau,t,\sigma(h)y},\quad\forall\tau\leq t,h\in\mathbb{R},y\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}.

Noting
Y_{t}:=\Phi_{t+h,\tau+h,y}(\omega,w_{0})
solves the equation

\displaystyle Y_{t}

\displaystyle=w_{0}+\int_{\tau+h}^{t+h}\left(\nu\Delta Y_{r-h}-B(\mathcal{K}Y_{r-h},Y_{r-h})\right)dr+\int_{\tau+h}^{t+h}f(y+r)dr+G(W(t+h,\omega)-W(\tau+h,\omega))

\displaystyle=w_{0}+\int_{\tau}^{t}\left(\nu\Delta Y_{r}-B(\mathcal{K}Y_{r},Y_{r})\right)dr+\int_{\tau}^{t}f(y+r+h)dr+G(W(t,\theta_{h}\omega)-W(\tau,\theta_{h}\omega)).

And
w_{t}=\Phi_{t,\tau,\sigma(h)y}(\omega,w_{0})
solves the equation

\displaystyle w_{t}=w_{0}+\int_{\tau}^{t}\left(\nu\Delta w_{r}-B(\mathcal{K}w_{r},w_{r})\right)dr+\int_{\tau}^{t}f(y+r+h)dr+G(W(t,\omega)-W(\tau,\omega)).

It then follows from the uniqueness of solution that

(3.5)

\displaystyle\Phi_{t+h,\tau+h,y}(\omega,w_{0})=\Phi_{t,\tau,\sigma(h)y}(\theta_{h}\omega,w_{0}).

The fact that
\mathbf{P}
is invariant under the Wiener shift
\theta_{t}
implies that

\mathcal{P}_{\tau+h,t+h,y}=\mathcal{P}_{\tau,t,\sigma(h)y},

which is the desired translation identity.

Let
R>0
and
g(w)=V^{2\kappa}(w)=\exp\left({2\kappa\eta}\|w\|^{2}\right)
, where the Lyapunov function
V(w)=e^{\eta\|w\|^{2}}
and
\kappa,\eta
are from Proposition
3.1
. To apply the fixed point theorem, we first consider a family of closed subsets
\mathcal{P}_{R}
of
\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)
indexed by
R>0
, which is defined by

\mathcal{P}_{R}:=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(H):\int_{H}g(w)\mu(dw)\leq R\},\quad R>0.

For each fixed
R
,
\mathcal{P}_{R}
is indeed a closed subset of the complete space
\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)
defined as in (
1.4
). For any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{R}
, one has

\rho(\mu,\delta_{0})=\int_{H}\rho(w,0)\mu(dw)\leq\int_{H}\|w\|e^{\eta\|w\|^{2}}\mu(dw)\leq C\int_{H}g(w)\mu(dw)<\infty,

so that
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)
. Let
\mu_{n}
be a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{P}_{R}
under the metric
\rho
. Then there is a unique
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)
such that
\rho(\mu_{n},\mu)\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. So
\mu_{n}
converges to
\mu
weakly. For
N>0
, let
g_{N}(w)=\min\{g(w),N\}
, then
g_{N}\in C_{b}(H)
, and

\int_{H}g_{N}(w)\mu(dw)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{H}g_{N}(w)\mu_{n}(dw)\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{H}g(w)\mu_{n}(dw)\leq R.

Hence by the monotone convergence theorem, one has
\int_{H}g(w)\mu(dw)\leq R
. Therefore
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{R}
, which shows that
\mathcal{P}_{R}
is closed.

Next we show that for any
t_{0}>0
, there is some
R_{t_{0}}>0
such that for any
t\geq t_{0}
,
\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}^{*}
maps
\mathcal{P}_{R_{t_{0}}}
to itself. It follows from Proposition
3.1
that for
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{R}
,

\displaystyle\int_{H}g_{N}(w)\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}^{*}\mu(dw)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{H}\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}g(w)\mu(dw)

\displaystyle=\int_{H}\mathbf{E}g(\mathrm{\Phi}_{s,s+t}(w))\mu(dw)\leq C\int_{H}g^{\alpha(t)}(w)\mu(dw)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\int_{H}g(w)\mu(dw)\right)^{\alpha(t)}\leq CR^{\alpha(t_{0})},

where we used Jensen’s inequality in the penultimate step. Hence

(3.6)

\displaystyle\int_{H}g(w)\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}^{*}\mu(dw)\leq CR^{\alpha(t_{0})}

again by monotone convergence theorem. In words, for any
R>0
, and
t\geq t_{0}
, the transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}^{*}
maps
\mathcal{P}_{R}
to
\mathcal{P}_{CR^{\alpha(t_{0})}}
. If we choose
R=R_{t_{0}}:=C^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha(t_{0})}}
, then
CR_{t_{0}}^{\alpha(t_{0})}=R_{t_{0}}
, therefore
\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}^{*}
maps
\mathcal{P}_{R_{t_{0}}}
to itself.

Now we show that for fixed
s\in\mathbb{R},t>0
and
\mu
satisfying
\int_{H}g(w)\mu(dw)<\infty
,
\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t,y}^{*}\mu
is continuous in
y
. Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in H(f)
and
w_{s,s+t}^{f_{i}}(w)
be the corresponding unique solution of equation (
1.1
) with
f
replaced by
f_{i}
, then by the bound (
6.3
), there is constant
\eta_{0}>0
so that for
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, there are constants
C,\gamma>0
such that

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\|w_{s,s+t}^{f_{1}}(w)-w_{s,s+t}^{f_{2}}(w)\|^{2}\leq Ce^{\gamma t}e^{\eta\|w\|^{2}}\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{\infty}^{2}.

From the definition of
\rho
as in (
1.3
), one has

(3.8)

\displaystyle\rho(w_{1},w_{2})\leq\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|\left(e^{\eta\|w_{1}\|^{2}}+e^{\eta\|w_{2}\|^{2}}\right),\quad\forall w_{1},w_{2}\in H.

It is known
[
8
,
55
]
that for any
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(H)
,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\rho(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\inf\mathbf{E}\rho(X_{1},X_{2}),

where the infimum is taken over all couplings
(X_{1},X_{2})
for
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
.
Combining (
3.7
)-(
3.9
) with bounds (
6.1
), it follows that

\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t,f_{1}}^{*}\delta_{w},\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t,f_{2}}^{*}\delta_{w})\leq\mathbf{E}\rho(w_{s,s+t}^{f_{1}}(w),w_{s,s+t}^{f_{2}}(w))

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathbf{E}\|w_{s,s+t}^{f_{1}}(w)-w_{s,s+t}^{f_{2}}(w)\|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(2\mathbf{E}\left[\exp({2\eta w_{s,s+t}^{f_{1}}(w)})+\exp({2\eta w_{s,s+t}^{f_{2}}(w)})\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{\gamma t}g(w)\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{\infty}.

Therefore by the Markov property,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t,f_{1}}^{*}\mu,\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t,f_{2}}^{*}\mu)\leq Ce^{\gamma t}\int_{H}g(w)\mu(dw)\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{\infty}.

Since
\int_{H}g(w)\mu(dw)<\infty
, we see that
\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t,y}^{*}\mu
is continuous in
y
.
However, it is not clear if
\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t,y}^{*}\mu
is continuous in
y
for any fixed
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)
.
In addition, in view of (
3.6
), we know that for fixed
R>0
, the transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}^{*}
is not necessarily contracting on
\mathcal{P}_{R}
for all
t>0
. Hence we choose to work with the family of closed subsets
\left\{\mathcal{P}_{R}\right\}_{R>0}
.

Observe that by Theorem
2.2
, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\geq 0
,
\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}^{*}
maps
\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)
to itself. So by the translation identity (
3.4
),
\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t,y}^{*}
maps
\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)
to itself since for any
y\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}
there is some
s_{y}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
y=\sigma(s_{y})0
. Denote for convenience

\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)\times\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}(H),\text{ by }\varphi(t,\mu,y)=\mathcal{P}_{0,t,y}^{*}\mu.

By the contraction property in Theorem
2.2
and translation identity (
3.4
), we have that for any
R>0
and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{R}
,
\varphi
is continuous in
\mu
, uniformly with respect to
y
. And by inequality (
3.10
), it is continuous in
y
uniformly for
\mu
. Hence
\varphi
is joint continuous in
(\mu,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{R}\times\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}
. Moreover,
\varphi
has the cocycle property over the base dynamical system
(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathbb{R},\sigma)
since for all
\tau,t\geq 0
and
y\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}
,
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)
,

\mathcal{P}_{0,t+\tau,y}^{*}\mu=\mathcal{P}_{t,t+\tau,y}^{*}\mathcal{P}_{0,t,y}^{*}\mu=\mathcal{P}_{0,\tau,\sigma(t)y}^{*}\mathcal{P}_{0,t,y}^{*}\mu.

Inspired by
[
7
]
, we consider the set of all continuous sections
C(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathcal{P}_{1}(H))
, on which we define the metric

p(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}):=\max_{y\in{\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}}}\rho(\gamma_{1}(y),\gamma_{2}(y)),

for
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in C(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathcal{P}_{1}(H))
, which is complete under this metric since
(\mathcal{P}_{1}(H),\rho)
is complete.

Fix
t_{0}\in(0,1)
. For
t\geq t_{0}
and
\gamma\in C(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathcal{P}_{R_{t_{0}}})
, define
S^{t}:C(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathcal{P}_{R_{t_{0}}})\rightarrow C(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathcal{P}_{R_{t_{0}}})
by

S^{t}(\gamma)(y)=\varphi(t,\gamma(\sigma(-t)y),\sigma(-t)y)).

Since
\varphi
maps
\mathcal{P}_{R_{t_{0}}}
to itself,
S^{t}
is well defined for
t\geq t_{0}
.
The cocycle property of
\varphi
in turn implies that
S^{t_{1}}S^{t_{2}}=S^{t_{1}+t_{2}}
for
t_{1},t_{2}\geq t_{0}
. Indeed, if we denote
\gamma^{\prime}=S^{t_{2}}(\gamma)
, then one has

\displaystyle S^{t_{1}}S^{t_{2}}\gamma(y)

\displaystyle=\varphi(t_{1},\gamma^{\prime}(\sigma(-t_{1})y),\sigma(-t_{1})y)

\displaystyle=\varphi(t_{1},\varphi(t_{2},\gamma(\sigma(-t_{1}-t_{2})y),\sigma(-t_{1}-t_{2})y)),\sigma(t_{2})\sigma(-t_{1}-t_{2})y)

\displaystyle=\varphi(t_{1}+t_{2},\gamma(\sigma(-t_{1}-t_{2})y),\sigma(-t_{1}-t_{2})y)=S^{t_{1}+t_{2}}\gamma(y),

where in the penultimate step we used the cocycle property.

Now note that by Theorem
2.2
and translation identity (
3.4
), one has

\displaystyle p(S^{t}\gamma_{1},S^{t}\gamma_{2})

\displaystyle=\max_{y\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}}\rho(\varphi(t,\gamma_{1}(\sigma(-t)y),\sigma(-t)y),\varphi(t,\gamma_{2}(\sigma(-t)y),\sigma(-t)y))

\displaystyle=\max_{y\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}}\rho(\mathcal{P}_{0,t,y}^{*}\gamma_{1}(\sigma(-t)y),\mathcal{P}_{0,t,y}^{*}\gamma_{2}(\sigma(-t)y))

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\gamma t}\max_{y\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}}}\rho(\gamma_{1}(\sigma(-t)y),\gamma_{2}(\sigma(-t)y))=Ce^{-\gamma t}p(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}).

Therefore for large
T>t_{0}
,
p(S^{T}\gamma_{1},S^{T}\gamma_{2})\leq cp(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
for some
c\in(0,1)
. Fix such a
T
, then
S^{T}
is a contraction over the complete metric space
C(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathcal{P}_{R_{t_{0}}})
, so there is a unique fixed point
\Gamma_{t_{0}}\in C(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathcal{P}_{R_{t_{0}}})
of
S^{T}
. Noting for any
t\geq t_{0}
,
S^{t}
maps
C(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathcal{P}_{R_{t_{0}}})
to itself, hence

S^{T}(S^{t}\Gamma_{t_{0}})=S^{t}(S^{T}\Gamma_{t_{0}})=S^{t}(\Gamma_{t_{0}})

implies that
S^{t}(\Gamma_{t_{0}})=\Gamma_{t_{0}}
by uniqueness of the fixed point, which shows that
\Gamma_{t_{0}}
is a fixed point of
S^{t}
for
t\geq t_{0}
. For
0<t_{1}\leq t_{0}
, one has
R_{t_{1}}\geq R_{t_{0}}
, so
\mathcal{P}_{R_{t_{0}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{R_{t_{1}}}
. And for the same
T>0
,
S^{T}
is a contraction on
C(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathcal{P}_{R_{t_{1}}})
, which has a unique fixed point
\Gamma_{t_{1}}
. By uniqueness,
\Gamma_{t_{1}}=\Gamma_{t_{0}}
, hence
\Gamma_{t_{0}}
is also a fixed point of
S^{t}
for
t\geq t_{1}
. Since
t_{1}
is arbitrary, we see that
\Gamma:=\Gamma_{t_{0}}
is a fixed point of
S^{t}
for
t\geq 0
, that is,
\varphi(t,\Gamma(\sigma(-t)y),\sigma(-t)y)=\Gamma(y)
for all
y\in Y
. Replacing
y
with
\sigma(t)y
we have
\varphi(t,\Gamma(y),y)=\Gamma(\sigma(t)y)
which by definition is

\mathcal{P}_{0,t,y}^{*}\Gamma(y)=\Gamma(\sigma(t)y).

Replacing
y
by
\sigma(s)0
, and using the translation identity (
3.5
), we have
\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}^{*}\Gamma(\sigma(s)0)=\Gamma(\sigma(s+t)0)
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\geq 0
. Hence
\mu_{s}:=\Gamma(\sigma(s)0)
is a periodic invariant measure for the transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}
.
If there is another periodic invariant measure
\widetilde{\mu}_{s}
, then by Theorem
2.2
, we have that for any
s\in\mathbb{R},n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\rho(\mu_{s},\widetilde{\mu}_{s})=\rho(\mu_{s+n\mathcal{T}},\widetilde{\mu}_{s+n\mathcal{T}})=\rho(\mathcal{P}_{s,s+n\mathcal{T}}^{*}\mu_{s},\mathcal{P}_{s,s+n\mathcal{T}}^{*}\widetilde{\mu}_{s})\leq Ce^{-\gamma n\mathcal{T}}\rho(\mu_{s},\widetilde{\mu}_{s}).

Letting
n\rightarrow\infty
, we see that
\rho(\mu_{s},\widetilde{\mu}_{s})=0
and hence
\mu_{s}=\widetilde{\mu}_{s}
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
. So the periodic invariant measure is unique.
The exponentially contraction (
3.3
) under the Wasserstein metric
\rho
then follows from Theorem
2.2
and the invariance of
\mu_{s}
.
∎

The following result is equivalent to Theorem
3.2
but represented by the action of the transition operator on Lipschitz observables. From the unique periodic invariant measure, one can define a family of norms
\|\cdot\|_{\rho,s}
by

(3.11)

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{\rho,s}=\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(\phi)+\left|\mu_{s}(\phi)\right|,\quad s\in\mathbb{R},

where
\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(\phi)=\sup_{u\neq v}\frac{|\phi(u)-\phi(v)|}{\rho(u,v)}
is the Lipschitz constant and
\mu(\phi)=\int_{H}\phi(u)\mu(du)
for
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(H)
and
\phi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(H)
. Unlike in
[
35
]
, the norm here depends on the initial time to adapt to the time inhomogeneity.

Corollary 3.3
.

Assume
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
,
A_{\infty}=H
, there is a constant
\eta_{0}>0
, such that for every
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
. Then there exist constants
C,\gamma>0
such that

\|\mathcal{P}_{s,t}\phi-\mu_{t}(\phi)\|_{\rho,s}\leq Ce^{-\gamma(t-s)}\|\phi-\mu_{s}(\phi)\|_{\rho,s},

for every Fréchet differentiable
\phi:H\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
s\leq t
.

Proof.

By the Monge-Kantorovich daulity
[
8
,
55
]
and the contraction on
\mathcal{P}(H)
from Theorem
2.2
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{P}_{s,t}\phi(u)-\mathcal{P}_{s,t}\phi(v)|

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(\phi)\sup_{\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(\varphi)\leq 1}\left|\int_{H}\varphi(z)\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}\delta_{u}(dz)-\int_{H}\varphi(z)\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}\delta_{v}(dz)\right|

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(\phi)\rho(\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}\delta_{u},\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{*}\delta_{v})\leq\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(\phi)Ce^{-\gamma(t-s)}\rho(u,v),

for any
u,v\in H
. By the invariance of the periodic invariant measure from Theorem
3.2
,
\int_{H}(\mathcal{P}_{s,t}\phi-\mu_{t}(\phi))\mu_{s}(du)=0
. Therefore

\|\mathcal{P}_{s,t}\phi-\mu_{t}(\phi)\|_{\rho,s}=\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(\mathcal{P}_{s,t}\phi)\leq\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(\phi)Ce^{-\gamma(t-s)}=Ce^{-\gamma(t-s)}\|\phi-\mu_{s}(\phi)\|_{\rho,s}.

∎

In the following we will prove the convergence (
2.5
), i.e., the following

Theorem 3.4
.

Assume
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
,
A_{\infty}=H
. Then there is a constant
\eta_{0}>0
, such that for every
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, there exist constants
C,\gamma>0
independent of initial time
s
, such that for every
\phi\in C_{\eta}^{1}
as in (
2.3
),

(3.12)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi-\int_{H}\phi(w)\mu_{s+t}(dw)\|_{\eta}\leq Ce^{-\gamma t}\|\phi\|_{\eta},

for any
s\in\mathbb{R},t\geq 0
.

The proof of the theorem will be given at the end of this section by combining Corollary
3.3
and the quasi-equivalence of
\|\cdot\|_{\rho,s}
with
\|\cdot\|_{\eta}
that will be proved below.

In the time homogeneous case as in
[
35
]
, the exponentially mixing in an equivalent form that involves observables similar to (
2.5
) is given by proving the quasi-equivalence under the Markov semigroup of
\|\cdot\|_{\eta}
with an appropriate norm
\|\cdot\|_{\rho}
on
\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(H)
. The norm
\|\cdot\|_{\rho}
is actually a combination of
\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(\phi)
for
\phi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(H)
and the integral of
\phi
with respect to the unique invariant measure. In the present time inhomogeneous setting, the periodic invariant measure depends on time. Therefore, to show the mixing property (
2.5
), we choose the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\rho,s}
on
\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(H)
defined as above. This is natural since
\|\cdot\|_{\rho,s}
is quasi-equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{\eta}
under the transition operator
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}
and
\mathcal{P}_{s,t}
has a similar contraction property as in the time homogeneous case proved in Theorem 4.3
[
35
]
. The dependence on initial time of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\rho,s}
can be regarded as a property that adapts to the time inhomogeneity, to yield a uniform contraction under the action of the transition operator, see Theorem
3.7
below.

To begin with, we first define a family of auxiliary norms for
r\in[0,1]
. The first involves the Lipschitz constant in terms of the metric
\rho_{r}
given in (
2.2
). Define

\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r},s}:=\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho_{r}}(\phi)+\left|\mu_{s}(\phi)\right|,\quad s\in\mathbb{R},

where
\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho_{r}}(\phi)=\sup_{u\neq v}\frac{|\phi(u)-\phi(v)|}{\rho_{r}(u,v)}
. When
r=1
, it is the norm given as (
3.11
). The second one is a norm weighted by the Lyapunov function
V(w)=e^{\eta\|w\|}
, which was introduced in
[
35
]
.

(3.13)

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}:=\sup_{w\in H}\frac{|\phi(w)|+\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}.

Note when
r=1
,
\|\cdot\|_{V^{r}}=\|\cdot\|_{\eta}
. We first show that
\|\cdot\|_{\rho_{r},s}
can be bounded by
\|\cdot\|_{V^{r}}
from both sides with different values of
r
.

Proposition 3.5
.

There is a constant
C>0
such that

(3.14)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\|\phi\|_{V^{\kappa r}}\leq\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r},s}\leq C\|\phi\|_{V^{r}},

for
r\in[r_{0},1]
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\phi\in C^{1}(H)
, where the constants
0<r_{0}<1
and
\kappa>1
are taken from the Lyapunov structure in Proportion
3.1
.

Before giving the proof, we need a lemma that connects the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\rho_{r},s}
with the derivative part of
\|\cdot\|_{V^{r}}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For every
\phi\in C^{1}(H)
, we have

\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r},s}=\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}+\left|\mu_{s}(\phi)\right|,\quad s\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

We first claim that for
v\in H
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\sup_{u:\|u-v\|<\varepsilon}\frac{|\phi(u)-\phi(v)|}{\rho_{r}(u,v)}=\frac{\|\nabla\phi(v)\|}{V^{r}(v)}.

By definition of
\rho_{r}
as in (
2.2
),

\rho_{r}(u,v)\leq\int_{0}^{1}V^{r}\big((1-\tau)u+\tau v\big)\|u-v\|d\tau,

hence we have

\sup_{u:\|u-v\|<\varepsilon}\frac{|\phi(u)-\phi(v)|}{\rho_{r}(u,v)}\geq\sup_{u:\|u-v\|<\varepsilon}\frac{|\phi(u)-\phi(v)|}{\|u-v\|}\left(\sup_{u:\|u-v\|<\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{1}V^{r}\big((1-\tau)u+\tau v\big)d\tau\right)^{-1}.

Therefore by taking limit,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\sup_{u:\|u-v\|<\varepsilon}\frac{|\phi(u)-\phi(v)|}{\rho_{r}(u,v)}\geq\frac{\|\nabla\phi(v)\|}{V^{r}(v)}.

Next we prove the reverse inequality of (
3.15
). For fixed
v\in H
and any
u
satisfying
\|u-v\|<\varepsilon
, let
R>0
large such that
u,v\in B_{R}(0)
, the ball in
(H,\|\cdot\|)
with radius
R
centered at
0
. For any
w_{1},w_{2}\in B_{R}(0)
, one has the equivalence of metrics

\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|\leq\rho_{r}(w_{1},w_{2})\leq V(R)\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|.

Let
K=V(R)
. Then for any
\delta>0
, there exists a differentiable path
\gamma
connecting
u,v
such that

\rho_{r}(u,v)\leq\int_{0}^{1}V^{r}(\gamma(\tau))\|\dot{\gamma}(\tau)\|d\tau\leq\rho_{r}(u,v)+\delta\leq K\varepsilon+\delta.

Now for any
t\in[0,1]
,

\|\gamma(t)-v\|=\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\dot{\gamma}(\tau)d\tau\right\|\leq\int_{0}^{1}V^{r}(\gamma(\tau))\|\dot{\gamma}(\tau)\|d\tau\leq K\varepsilon+\delta,

which means that
\gamma(t)
never leaves the ball of radius
K\varepsilon+\delta
centered at
v
. Therefore,

\rho_{r}(u,v)\geq\int_{0}^{1}V^{r}(\gamma(\tau))\|\dot{\gamma}(\tau)\|d\tau-\delta\geq\inf_{w:\|w-v\|\leq K\varepsilon+\delta}V^{r}(w)\|u-v\|-\delta.

Hence by taking
\delta=\varepsilon\|u-v\|
above, we have

\displaystyle\frac{|\phi(u)-\phi(v)|}{\rho_{r}(u,v)}\leq\frac{|\phi(u)-\phi(v)|}{\|u-v\|}\left(\inf_{w:\|w-v\|\leq(K+\varepsilon)\varepsilon}V^{r}(w)-\varepsilon\right)^{-1}.

By taking limit we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\sup_{u:\|u-v\|<\varepsilon}\frac{|\phi(u)-\phi(v)|}{\rho_{r}(u,v)}\leq\frac{\|\nabla\phi(v)\|}{V^{r}(v)},

which finishes the proof of the claim.

It then follows from the claim that

(3.16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho_{r}}(\phi)=\sup_{u\neq v}\frac{|\phi(u)-\phi(v)|}{\rho_{r}(u,v)}\geq\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}.

Hence
\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r},s}\geq\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}+\left|\mu_{s}(\phi)\right|
. It remains to show the reverse inequality of (
3.16
). Without loss of generality we can assume
\phi(0)=0
and
\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho_{r}}(\phi)=1
. There is nothing to show if for some
w
,
1\leq\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}
. So we assume
\|\nabla\phi(w)\|\leq V^{r}(w)
for all
w
. Then for any
w_{1},w_{2}\in H
,

|\phi(w_{1})-\phi(w_{2})|=\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle\nabla\phi(\gamma(\tau)),\dot{\gamma}(\tau)\right\rangle d\tau\leq\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}\int_{0}^{1}V^{r}(\gamma(\tau))\|\dot{\gamma}(\tau)\|d\tau.

By taking infimum over all differentiable
\gamma
connecting
w_{1},w_{2}
, we have

\frac{|\phi(w_{1})-\phi(w_{2})|}{\rho_{r}(w_{1},w_{2})}\leq\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}.

The proof is then complete.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.5
.

By Theorem
3.2
, there is a constant
C>0
, independent of the initial time
s
such that

(3.17)

\displaystyle|\mu_{s}(\phi)|\leq\int_{H}V^{r}(w)\frac{|\phi(w)|}{V^{r}(w)}\mu_{s}(dw)\leq\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}\mu_{s}(V^{r})\leq C\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}.

Combining (
3.17
) with Lemma
3.6
and definition of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{r}
as (
3.13
), we have

\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r},s}\leq\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}+C\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}\leq\widetilde{C}\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}.

To show the first inequality of (
3.14
), we fix
\phi
with
\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r},s}=1.
Then by Proposition
6.3
,

|\phi(w)-\phi(0)|\leq\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho_{r}}(\phi)\rho_{r}(w,0)\leq\int_{0}^{1}V^{r}(\tau w)\|w\|d\tau\leq\|w\|V^{r}(w)\leq CV^{\kappa r}(w).

Also by Theorem
3.2
and noting that
r\leq 1
, we have

\int_{H}\rho_{r}(w,0)\mu_{s}(dw)\leq\int_{H}\rho(w,0)\mu_{s}(dw)\leq\int_{H}CV^{\kappa}(w)\mu_{s}(dw)\leq\widetilde{C}.

Hence

\displaystyle|\phi(0)|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{H}\phi(w)\mu_{s}(dw)-\phi(0)\right|+\left|\int_{H}\phi(w)\mu_{s}(dw)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{H}|\phi(w)-\phi(0)|\mu_{s}(dw)+\left|\int_{H}\phi(w)\mu_{s}(dw)\right|\leq\widetilde{C}+1,

where
\left|\int_{H}\phi(w)\mu_{s}(dw)\right|\leq 1
since
\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r},s}=1.
It then follows that

|\phi(w)|\leq|\phi(0)|+|\phi(w)-\phi(0)|\leq\widetilde{C}V^{\kappa r}(w).

Note that
\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r},s}=1
also implies that
\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}\leq 1
, therefore

\|\phi\|_{V^{\kappa r}}\leq\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{\kappa r}(w)}+\sup_{w\in H}\frac{|\phi(w)|}{V^{\kappa r}(w)}\leq 1+\widetilde{C}\leq C\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r},s}.

The proof is complete.
∎

The following result shows that the transition operator has a contraction property under the norms
\|\cdot\|_{V^{r}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\rho_{r},s}
.

Theorem 3.7
.

There are constants
C,\gamma>0
such that

(3.18)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi\|_{V^{r(t)}}\leq Ce^{\gamma t}\|\phi\|_{V^{r}},

(3.19)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi\|_{\rho_{r(t),s}}\leq Ce^{\gamma t}\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r,s+t}},

for
r\in[r_{0},2\kappa]
,
s\in\mathbb{R},t>0
and
\phi\in C^{1}(H)
, where
r(t)=\max\{r\alpha(t),r_{0}\}
and
\alpha(t)
is from Proposition
6.3
.

Proof.

We first prove the inequalities for
t\in[0,\mathcal{T}]
. By Proposition
3.1
, we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi(w)\|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}\|\nabla\phi(\Phi_{s,s+t}(w))\|\|\nabla\Phi_{s,s+t}(w)\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}\mathbf{E}V^{r}(\Phi_{s,s+t}(w))\|\nabla\Phi_{s,s+t}(w)\|

(3.20)

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}V^{r\alpha(t)}(w)\leq C\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}V^{r\alpha(t)}(w).

It follows from the penultimate step that

(3.21)

\displaystyle\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi(w)\|}{V^{r\alpha(t)}(w)}\leq C\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}.

Also

(3.22)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi(w)|\leq\mathbf{E}|\phi|(\Phi_{s,s+t}(w))\leq\sup_{w\in H}\frac{|\phi(w)|}{V^{r}(w)}\mathbf{E}V^{r}(\Phi_{s,s+t}(w))\leq C\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}V^{r\alpha(t)}(w).

Combining the above two estimates (
3.20
) and (
3.22
), we see that
\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi\|_{V^{r(t)}}\leq C\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}
with
r(t)=r\alpha(t)
. Lemma
3.6
and the invariance of the unique periodic invariant measure, together with inequality (
3.21
) imply that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi\|_{\rho_{r(t)},s}

\displaystyle=\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi(w)\|}{V^{r(t)}(w)}+\left|\int_{H}\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi(w)\mu_{s}(dw)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{w\in H}\frac{\|\nabla\phi(w)\|}{V^{r}(w)}+\left|\int_{H}\phi(w)\mu_{s+t}(dw)\right|\leq C\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r},s+t},\quad t\in[0,\mathcal{T}].

The case for
t>\mathcal{T}
follows by iteration. From Proposition
3.1
, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, one has
\alpha(n\mathcal{T})=\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{n}
, so that
r(n\mathcal{T})=\max\{r\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{n},r_{0}\}
. By induction, we can show that

(3.23)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+n\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{r(n\mathcal{T})}}\leq C^{n}\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}.

Indeed, the base case for
n=1
has been proved. In particular, replacing
s
by
s+k\mathcal{T}
, it follows that

(3.24)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s+k\mathcal{T},s+(k+1)\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{r(\mathcal{T})}}\leq C\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}.

Assume that for
n=k
, inequality (
3.23
) is true for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
r\in[r_{0},2\kappa]
. Then since
r(\mathcal{T})\in[r_{0},2\kappa]
, one has

(3.25)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+k\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{\max\{r(\mathcal{T})\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{k},r_{0}\}}}\leq C^{k}\|\phi\|_{V^{r(\mathcal{T})}}.

It follows from (
3.24
), (
3.25
) and the evolution property of the transition operator that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+(k+1)\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{\max\{r(\mathcal{T})\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{k},r_{0}\}}}=\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+k\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{P}_{s+k\mathcal{T},s+(k+1)\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{\max\{r(\mathcal{T})\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{k},r_{0}\}}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{k}\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+k\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{r(\mathcal{T})}}\leq C^{k+1}\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}.

Since
r(\mathcal{T})=\max\{r\alpha(\mathcal{T}),r_{0}\}
, we find that
r((k+1)\mathcal{T})=\max\{r\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{k+1},r_{0}\}\geq{\max\{r(\mathcal{T})\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{k},r_{0}\}}
always holds. Hence

\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+n\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{r((k+1)\mathcal{T})}}\leq\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+(k+1)\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{\max\{r(\mathcal{T})\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{k},r_{0}\}}}\leq C^{k+1}\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}.

This completes the induction step. Hence (
3.23
) is true for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
t\geq\mathcal{T}
, there are unique
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\beta\in[0,\mathcal{T})
such that
t=k\mathcal{T}+\beta
. Since

r(\beta)\in[r_{0},2\kappa]
, it follows from (
3.23
) that

(3.26)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+k\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{\max\{r(\beta)\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{k},r_{0}\}}}\leq C^{k}\|\phi\|_{V^{r(\beta)}}.

Combining (
3.26
) with (
3.18
) for
\beta\in[0,\mathcal{T})
, and the fact that

r(t)=\max\{r\alpha(\beta)\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{k},r_{0}\}\geq\max\{r(\beta)\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{k},r_{0}\},

we obtain (
3.18
) for
t\geq\mathcal{T}
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi\|_{V^{r(t)}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+k\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{P}_{s+k\mathcal{T},s+k\mathcal{T}+\beta}\phi\|_{\max\{r(\beta)\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{k},r_{0}\}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{k}\|\mathcal{P}_{s+k\mathcal{T},s+k\mathcal{T}+\beta}\phi\|_{V^{r(\beta)}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{k+1}\|\phi\|_{V^{r}}\leq Ce^{\gamma t}\|\phi\|_{V^{r}},

by choosing appropriate constants
C,\gamma>0
since
k=\frac{t-\beta}{\mathcal{T}}
. The proof for (
3.19
) in the case
t\geq\mathcal{T}
is similar.

∎

Corollary 3.8
.

There exist
m>0
,
C=C(m)>0
such that

\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+m\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{r}}\leq C\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r},\tau}

for all
\phi\in C^{1}(H)
,
r\in(1-\alpha(\mathcal{T}),1]
and
s,\tau\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let
r_{n}=r_{0}+\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{n}\kappa r
, where
r_{0}
is from Proposition
3.1
and can be chosen to be arbitrarily close to
0
. Since
\alpha(\mathcal{T})<1
, we can choose a large
m
such that
\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{n}\kappa r<1
. Fix such an
m
. Then we can choose
r_{0}
small such that
r_{m}\leq r
and
r_{0}<\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{m}\kappa r
. As a result, we have by Theorem
3.7
and Proposition
3.5
, that for
r\in(1-\alpha(\mathcal{T}),1]
,

\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+m\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{r}}\leq\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+m\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{r_{m}}}\leq\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+m\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{V^{\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{m}\kappa r}}\leq C(m)\|\phi\|_{\kappa r}\leq C(m)\|\phi\|_{\rho_{r},\tau},

where in the penultimate step we use inequality (
3.18
) with

r(t)=\max\{\kappa r\alpha(m\mathcal{T}),r_{0}\}=\max\{\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{m}\kappa r,r_{0}\}=\alpha(\mathcal{T})^{m}\kappa r.

∎

We are now in a position to prove Theorem
3.4
.

Proof of Theorem
3.4
.

By Corollary
3.3
, and Proposition
3.5
, we have

\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi-\mu_{s+t}(\phi)\|_{\rho,s}\leq Ce^{-\gamma t}\|\phi-\mu_{s}(\phi)\|_{\rho,s}\leq Ce^{-\gamma t}\|\phi-\mu_{s}(\phi)\|_{\eta},

for
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\geq 0
. By Corollary
3.8
, there exists
m>0
such that

\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+m\mathcal{T}}\phi\|_{\eta}\leq C(m)\|\phi\|_{\rho,\tau}.

Replacing
\phi
by
\mathcal{P}_{s+m\mathcal{T},s+m\mathcal{T}+t}\phi-\mu_{s+m\mathcal{T}+t}(\phi)
and letting
\tau=s+m\mathcal{T}
on the right hand side of the above inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+m\mathcal{T}+t}\phi-\mu_{s+m\mathcal{T}+t}(\phi)\|_{\eta}

\displaystyle\leq C(m)\|\mathcal{P}_{s+m\mathcal{T},s+m\mathcal{T}+t}\phi-\mu_{s+m\mathcal{T}+t}(\phi)\|_{\rho,s+m\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle\leq C(m)e^{-\gamma t}\|\phi-\mu_{s+m\mathcal{T}}(\phi)\|_{\eta}.

Combining the above estimate with (
3.17
), one has for
t\geq m\mathcal{T}
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi-\mu_{s+t}(\phi)\|_{\eta}\leq Ce^{-\gamma(t-m\mathcal{T})}\left(\|\phi\|_{\eta}+|\mu_{s+m\mathcal{T}}(\phi)|\right)\leq Ce^{-\gamma t}\|\phi\|_{\eta},

where
C
depends on the constants
m,\mathcal{T}
.

By Theorem
3.7
, we have for all
t>0
,

\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi\|_{\eta}=\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi\|_{V^{1}}\leq\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi\|_{V^{\max\{\alpha(t),r_{0}\}}}\leq Ce^{\gamma t}\|\phi\|_{\eta}.

So for
0\leq t\leq m\mathcal{T}
,
\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi\|_{\eta}\leq Ce^{\gamma m\mathcal{T}}\|\phi\|_{\eta}
. Replacing
\phi
by
\phi-\mu_{s+t}(\phi)
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{s,s+t}\phi-\mu_{s+t}(\phi)\|_{\eta}\leq Ce^{\gamma m\mathcal{T}}\|\phi-\mu_{s+t}(\phi)\|_{\eta}\leq Ce^{\gamma m\mathcal{T}}\|\phi\|_{\eta}\leq Ce^{-\gamma t}\|\phi\|_{\eta},

by choosing the last constant
C
larger. The proof is complete.
∎

3.2.
The weak irreducibility

In this subsection, we show that the Navier-Stokes system (
1.1
) has the following form of weak irreducibility,
which is necessary to obtain the desired contraction on
\mathcal{P}(H)
.

Proposition 3.9

(Weak Irreducibility)
.

If the Grashof number
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
, then for any
R,\varepsilon>0,r\in(0,1]
, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\geq n_{0}
, there exits
a>0
so that

\inf_{\|w_{1}\|,\|w_{2}\|\leq R}\sup_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{C}\left(\mathcal{P}_{s,s+n\mathcal{T}}^{*}\delta_{w_{1}},\mathcal{P}_{s,s+n\mathcal{T}}^{*}\delta_{w_{2}}\right)}\Gamma\left\{(w_{1}^{\prime},w_{2}^{\prime})\in H\times H:\rho_{r}(w_{1}^{\prime},w_{2}^{\prime})<\varepsilon\right\}\geq a,

for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, where the metric
\rho_{r}
is given by (
2.2
).

As we mentioned in the introduction, to ensure the weak irreducibility in our work, we impose a condition on the Grashof number
G_{1}
instead of the range condition. Under this condition
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
, there is a unique stable periodic solution
z(t)
of the deterministic equation corresponding to equation (
1.1
), see
[
14
]
. From this fact we can show that any two solutions of equation (
1.1
) starting from different initial points at the same initial time
s
have a positive probability to be attracted by the periodic solution
z(t)
. Since the driven force
f(t,x)
is periodic, the solutions will recurrently enter into smaller neighborhood of the point
z(s)
at periodic times
s+n\mathcal{T}
for large
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This gives us the weak irreducibility as in Proposition
3.9
, which is an adaptation of the weak irreducibility introduced in
[
35
]
to the time inhomogeneous and periodic setting. The proof is based on the following Lemma
3.10
and Lemma
3.11
.

For
\delta>0,T>s
, we let

\mathrm{\Omega}_{\delta,T}=\left\{\omega\in\mathrm{\Omega}:|W(\omega,t)|\leq\delta,\quad\text{for all}\quad t\in[s,T]\right\},

where
|\cdot|
denote the usual norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then by the property of Wiener process, there exists
\delta^{*}=\delta^{*}(\delta,T)
such that

(3.27)

\mathbf{P}(\mathrm{\Omega}_{\delta,T})\geq\delta^{*}>0.

Let
V_{t}
be the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process given by

\partial_{t}V_{t}=\nu\mathrm{\Delta}V_{t}+GdW_{t},\quad V(s)=0.

By viewing the process
V_{t}
as a stochastic convolution, one has the following

Lemma 3.10
.

[
21
]

For any
\delta>0,T>s
, there exists a deterministic constant
\varepsilon_{\delta,T}>0
such that
\varepsilon_{\delta,T}\rightarrow 0
as
\delta\rightarrow 0
for
T
fixed, and

(3.28)

\sup_{t\in[s,T]}\left\|V(t,\omega)\right\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon_{\delta,T},\text{ for all}\quad\omega\in\mathrm{\Omega}_{\delta,T}.

Recall that
w_{s,t}:=w(t,\omega;s,w_{0})
denotes the solution to the Navier-Stokes equation (
1.1
). Observe that
Z_{t}:=w_{s,t}-V_{t}
solves the random PDE perturbed by the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process
V_{t}

\partial_{t}Z=\nu\mathrm{\Delta}Z-B\left(\mathcal{K}\left(Z+V_{t}\right),Z+V_{t}\right)+f(t),\quad Z(s)=w_{0}.

When the Grashof number
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
, there exists a unique
\mathcal{T}
-periodic solution
z_{t}
(see
[
14
]
) of the corresponding unperturbed deterministic equation

(3.29)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}z=\nu\mathrm{\Delta}z-B(\mathcal{K}z,z)+f(t),\quad z(s)=z_{s}.

Then
u_{t}:=Z_{t}-z_{t}
solves the equation

\partial_{t}u=\nu\mathrm{\Delta}u-B\left(\mathcal{K}\left(Z_{t}+V_{t}\right),Z_{t}+V_{t}\right)+B(\mathcal{K}z_{t},z_{t}),\quad u(s)=w_{0}-z_{s}

Note
-B\left(\mathcal{K}\left(Z_{t}+V_{t}\right),Z_{t}+V_{t}\right)+B(\mathcal{K}z_{t},z_{t})=-B\left(\mathcal{K}\left(u+V_{t}\right),u+V_{t}+z_{t}\right)-B(\mathcal{K}z_{t},u+V_{t})
. So the equation for
u_{t}
is actually

(3.30)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\nu\mathrm{\Delta}u-B\left(\mathcal{K}\left(u+V_{t}\right),u+V_{t}+z_{t}\right)-B(\mathcal{K}z_{t},u+V_{t}),\quad u(s)=w_{0}-z_{s}.

In what follows, we will estimate the
H
norm of
u
from the above equation so that one can see the attraction property of
z_{t}
when the perturbation
V_{t}
is sufficiently small.

The following lemma shows that there is a positive probability such that, uniformly for initial values in any bounded region, the process
u_{t}
can eventually enter the ball centered at the origin with arbitrary small radius.

Lemma 3.11
.

Suppose
G_{1}<1/c_{0}
. For arbitrary
R,\sigma>0
, there exists
\delta=\delta(R,\sigma)>0,T_{0}=T_{0}(R,\sigma,\delta)>s
such that, for
T\geq T_{0}
,

(3.31)

\left\|u(T,u_{s})\right\|\leq\frac{\sigma}{2},\quad\text{on}\quad\mathrm{\Omega}_{\delta,T},

for any
u_{s}\in B_{R}(0)\subset H
.

Proof.

Taking
H
inner product of equation (
3.30
) with
u
, and using the fact that
\langle B(\mathcal{K}u,v),v\rangle=0
and
\left|\langle B(\mathcal{K}u,v),w\rangle\right|\leq c_{0}\|\mathcal{K}u\|_{1}\|v\|_{1}\|w\|_{1}=c_{0}\|u\|\|v\|_{1}\|w\|_{1}
for any
u,v,w\in H
, and the Poincare inequality
\|u\|\leq\|u\|_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\|u\|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\nu\|\nabla u\|^{2}-\langle B(\mathcal{K}u,V_{t}+z_{t}),u\rangle-\langle B(\mathcal{K}V_{t},V_{t}+z_{t}),u)-\langle B(\mathcal{K}z_{t},V_{t}),u)

\displaystyle\leq-\nu||u||_{1}^{2}+c_{0}\|u\|\|u\|_{1}\|z_{t}\|_{1}+c_{0}\|u\|_{1}^{2}\|V_{t}\|_{1}+c_{0}\|V_{t}\|_{1}^{2}\|u\|_{1}+2c_{0}\|V_{t}\|_{1}\|z_{t}\|_{1}\|u\|_{1}.

By Lemma
3.10
,
||V_{t}||_{1}\leq\varepsilon:=\varepsilon_{\delta,T}
for
\delta,T
that will be determined later. And from
[
13
]
, it is known that
\|z_{t}\|_{1}\leq R^{\prime}
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, where
R^{\prime}
is a constant depending on
\nu
and
\|f\|_{\infty}
. Therefore there exists a constant
C=C(\nu,c_{0},\|f\|_{\infty})
such that for fixed
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\|u\|^{2}+2\nu||u||_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2c_{0}\|u\|\|u\|_{1}\|z_{t}\|_{1}+2c_{0}\varepsilon^{2}\|u\|_{1}+2c_{0}\varepsilon\|u\|_{1}^{2}+4c_{0}\varepsilon\|z_{t}\|_{1}\|u\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}^{2}(\alpha\nu)^{-1}\|u\|^{2}\|z_{t}\|_{1}^{2}+\left(\alpha\nu+C\varepsilon\right)\|u\|_{1}^{2}+C\varepsilon.

Choosing
\delta
sufficiently small so that
\alpha\nu+C\varepsilon\leq\nu
, then we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\|u\|^{2}\leq-\left(\nu-c_{0}^{2}(\alpha\nu)^{-1}\|z_{t}\|_{1}^{2}\right)\|u\|^{2}+C\varepsilon.

By Gronwall’s inequality,

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|u_{s}\|^{2}\exp\left(-\int_{s}^{t}\left(\nu-c_{0}^{2}(\alpha\nu)^{-1}\|z_{\tau}\|_{1}^{2}\right)d\tau\right)+C\varepsilon\int_{s}^{t}\exp\left(-\int_{\tau}^{t}\left(\nu-c_{0}^{2}(\alpha\nu)^{-1}\|z_{r}\|_{1}^{2}\right)dr\right)d\tau

\displaystyle:=I_{1}+I_{2}.

Multiplying equation (
3.29
) by
z_{t}
and integrating over the torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, one has

(3.32)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\|z_{t}\|^{2}\leq-\nu\|z_{t}\|_{1}^{2}+\nu^{-1}\|f(t)\|^{2}.

Hence we deduce the energy inequality

(3.33)
